# Fixed Bugs Log

A changelog of bugs that were tracked in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md` and have since been fixed. `KNOWN_BUGS.md` only ever holds *open* items — an entry is removed from it outright the moment it's fixed, not marked done in place — so this file is where the record of "what it was and when it got fixed" lives instead. Git history has the exact diffs; this is a scannable index into that history, one entry per fix, newest first.

## 2026-10-07

### A-179 — every production table was MyISAM, so every transaction, savepoint and row lock in this codebase was a silent no-op
- **Was:** `config/database.php` set `'engine' => null`, and the host's `default_storage_engine` is **MyISAM** (`SHOW ENGINES` reports `MyISAM => DEFAULT`). So every `Schema::create` in this project's history inherited it, and `SELECT engine, COUNT(*) ... GROUP BY engine` returned exactly one row: `MyISAM => 63`.
- **Why it was invisible.** MyISAM does not reject a transaction, it ignores it. `beginTransaction()`, `commit()`, `rollBack()`, savepoints and `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` all silently do nothing. Meanwhile the test suite runs on **SQLite, which honours transactions** — so it is *more* capable than production here, and every test asserting rollback behaviour passed while the production guarantee was absent. Nothing in CI could have caught it.
- **What was actually broken in production:** `SyncController::push()`'s per-change savepoint isolation and PG-18's `$entryTransactionLevel` unwind (a push dying midway left partial writes committed); `SyncCommandService::pendingFor()`'s atomic claim (two devices could both act on one command); and `RegistersAccounts::register()`'s all-or-nothing signup, which the roadmap records as the fix for orphaned user rows — that fix had never worked on this host.
- **Found** by inference from a key-length failure, not by a report: a migration died with `max key length is 1000 bytes`, and 1000 is MyISAM/Aria's limit — InnoDB reports 767 or 3072, never 1000. See A-178b.
- **Fix:** `config/database.php` now pins `'engine' => 'InnoDB'`, guarded by `tests/Feature/DatabaseEngineIsPinnedTest.php` since a null engine fails invisibly; and both databases were converted table-by-table with `ALTER TABLE ... ENGINE=InnoDB ROW_FORMAT=DYNAMIC`, smallest-first, dev as a rehearsal before production. 63/63 on each, zero failures, verified `InnoDB => 63`.
- **`ROW_FORMAT=DYNAMIC` was load-bearing, not decoration.** `device_stock_reports` and `device_queue_reports` carry a 908-byte `unique(['store_id','device_id'])`, over InnoDB COMPACT's 767-byte cap. A bare `ENGINE=InnoDB` would have depended on `innodb_default_row_format`, which happens to be `dynamic` on this host — relying on that silently is how the original bug happened.
- **No foreign-key surprise.** MySQL parses and discards FK definitions on MyISAM, so those constraints were never created and there was nothing to enforce against existing data. This is why a conversion that sounds frightening was not.
- **Consequence to watch:** InnoDB's `COUNT(*)` without a `WHERE` is an index scan, where MyISAM read it from metadata in constant time. Any unbounded whole-table count is now slower in proportion to table size.

### A-178b — `create_sync_commands_table` could not be applied on production: a composite index four bytes over the key limit
- **Was:** `index(['store_id', 'device_id', 'status'])` on `sync_commands`. Under utf8mb4 that is `char(36)` 144 + `varchar(191)` 764 + `varchar(24)` 96 = **1004 bytes**, against the production server's 1000-byte limit. The migration failed on the live box after its two siblings had already applied; MySQL DDL is not transactional, so `sync_commands` was left created-without-its-index and its `migrations` row unrecorded, blocking every later migration.
- **No outage resulted.** `SyncController::exchangeSyncCommands()` catches `Throwable` and returns `[]`, and the table itself existed, so pushes and the admin panel kept working throughout. What was blocked was applying any further migration.
- **Fix:** `device_id` is now 64 rather than 191 — 496 bytes total, with real margin. A device id is a short machine identifier; 191 was inherited from the old 767-byte single-column rule and was never right for a three-column index.
- **Why 1165 passing tests said nothing.** The whole suite runs on SQLite, which has no key-length limit, so no test could have failed. There is no MySQL in CI either. The new guard is `tests/Feature/SchemaIndexKeyLengthTest.php`, which parses the migration **source** — SQLite reports a column type as a bare `varchar` with no length, so the migrated test schema cannot answer the question at all.
- **Two things that went wrong while writing that guard, both worth knowing.** The first version read the live schema and **passed vacuously** against the very migration that was broken, because every column fell through to a default width; it now asserts a floor on how many indexes it resolved, and asserts by name that the `sync_commands` index is in scope. The second version flagged a 1600-byte index in `create_inventory_system_tables` that is **commented out** — it now strips comments before parsing, because being sent to fix dead code is how a guard loses its credibility.
- **It left a bigger question open:** a 1000-byte limit is MyISAM/Aria, not InnoDB, which implies new tables are not getting InnoDB and that transactions may be silent no-ops. Logged as A-179 in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md` pending one query against production — deliberately not acted on from here.

### PG-17 — a device whose rows never reach the server is no longer invisible
- **Was:** the server recorded every push outcome it *received*, but `_sync_queue` is a client-side SQLite table, so a store sitting on 500 rows that were never transmitted looked identical to a healthy one. Logged as an explicit Phase 2 boundary ("Phase 2b"), not discovered after the fact.
- **Fix:** the client now reports its own queue state on every sync — depth, and for each stuck row the table, record id, attempt count and reason — stored per `(store_id, device_id)` and rendered on the store detail page. The spec is `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-07-stuck-data-and-divergence-visibility-design.md`; the architecture and its non-negotiable constraints are in `client/AGENTS.md` ("What a sync request reports about this device") and `web/AGENTS.md` ("Stuck data and divergence visibility").
- **Two things that were not obvious going in.** The reason has to be canonicalised **on the device**: a raw driver error embeds the failing SQL and its bindings — customer names, phone numbers, amounts — and canonicalising only on storage still puts that text in transit and in any request log. And the cap on reported items has to be a `LIMIT` in the SQL, not a `.slice()` afterwards, or a device with a large backlog pays the cost of building the full list on every sync.
- **A store that has never reported renders "no reports yet", never "0 divergence".** Absent and zero are the same pixels otherwise, and the silent device is exactly the one worth looking at.
- **Still true, and still a limit:** admin visibility lags client adoption, because a device only reports once it is running a build that knows how to. PG-17's original note about this stands.
- **Regression coverage.** `client/__tests__/queue-state-report.test.ts` (including that a raw driver error carrying a customer name and phone number never leaves the device), plus the server-side storage and store-detail rendering tests.

## 2026-10-06

### PG-12 — `AdminRevenueService::getOverview()` loaded every matching transaction to render twenty
- **Found:** logged while scoping admin Phase 1; fixed in Phase 3, which touches the same query.
- **What was wrong:** `->get()` on every `status = 'success'` row (with `subscription.user` eager-loaded), then `->slice()` of 20 into a hand-built `LengthAwarePaginator`. The totals, the per-currency breakdown and the by-plan-tier grouping were all computed by summing that in-memory collection. It violated `.agents/AGENTS.md` §8's pagination rule and degraded linearly with payment volume — and Phase 2 made it worse by adding `AdminSummaryService` as a second caller, so it ran on **every admin Overview load**, not just visits to the Marketing → Revenue tab.
- **Why it was written that way:** the original comment said `plan_name` lives in the `metadata` JSON column, so filtering and grouping had to happen in PHP. That was only half true — `planNameFor()` already *prefers* `$txn->subscription->plan_name` and falls back to metadata, so the filter and the grouping can run in SQL against a joined `subscriptions.plan_name` without any driver-specific JSON query.
- **Fix:** the plan filter became a `whereHas('subscription')` with a `LOWER(plan_name)` comparison; the money totals and the by-plan-tier breakdown became `GROUP BY` aggregates; and the row list became a real `->paginate(20)`. The metadata fallback survives for *display* of the page's rows only. Note `payment_transactions.status` had to be qualified once `subscriptions` is joined — both tables have a `status` column, and the unqualified form raised "ambiguous column name".
- **An orphaned transaction keeps its metadata plan for display and drops out of a `plan=` filter**, which is correct: with no subscription row there is no authoritative plan to filter on.
- **Regression coverage.** `tests/Feature/Admin/AdminRevenuePaginationTest.php`: the row-fetch query must carry a `LIMIT` (aggregates are excluded from that assertion, since a `GROUP BY` returns one row per group and is bounded by cardinality, not by transaction count); the plan filter still returns the right rows; the per-currency totals survive the rewrite; and an orphaned transaction still renders a plan while being excluded from a filter. **The first assertion was initially written as a query *count* and passed before the fix** — a full-table load is still one query — so it was rewritten to assert the absence of an unbounded row select, which fails on the old implementation and passes on the new one.

### PG-16 — the admin Overview's "Sync Success Rate" read an activity-log action that nothing in either package has ever written
- **Found:** while scoping admin Phase 2, by grepping for the metric's data source before building on it. `AdminSummaryService` queried `ActivityLog` for `action = 'SYNC_SUCCESS'` (and `action LIKE 'SYNC_%'`). A repo-wide grep across `laravel-server/` and `client/` found **exactly one reference to `SYNC_SUCCESS`: that read.** No code has ever written one.
- **What it meant in practice.** The row rendered "No sync activity" permanently. The pre-Phase-1 version of the same code had the same dead source but defaulted to the literal `'100%'`, so it displayed a confident fabrication instead — Phase 1 replaced a false number with an honest-but-permanently-empty one, which satisfied the letter of its own rule ("an unmeasurable metric renders as unavailable") while still shipping a dead panel row. The honest reading is that Phase 1 inherited the old code's assumption and windowed it without checking the source existed.
- **Fix.** Phase 2 records real push outcomes (`sync_health_daily`, see `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-admin-panel-phase-2-sync-health-design.md`) and `AdminSummaryService::syncSuccessRateToday()` now reads that table: `accepted / (accepted + refused)` over today's bucket, returning `null` when the denominator is zero. The null-when-no-data behaviour is unchanged and still pinned by `test_sync_success_rate_is_null_when_there_is_no_sync_activity_in_the_window`.
- **Regression coverage.** `test_the_sync_success_rate_reads_recorded_sync_health` asserts a non-null rate after a recorded push — the specific guard against shipping a third metric whose source nothing writes. `test_sync_success_rate_counts_only_todays_bucket` (renamed from the old 24-hour name) was rewritten against the new source rather than deleted, so the windowing is still pinned.

### PG-11 — `AdminRevenueService` summed `amount` across mixed `currency` values, so the admin Marketing → Revenue total stopped meaning anything the moment a non-NGN payment landed
- **Found:** while scoping the Admin Panel Phase 1 work (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-06-admin-panel-phase-1-design.md`), not via a report. Never logged in `KNOWN_BUGS.md` — found and fixed in the same pass, so it is recorded here directly.
- **What was wrong.** `getOverview()` computed `$totalRevenue = (float) $transactions->sum('amount')` and `$manualRevenue` the same way, over a collection whose rows each carry their own `payment_transactions.currency`. Adding ₦45,000 to GH₵300 to KES 400 yields `45700`, a number with no unit, which the Revenue tab then rendered through a local `formatNaira()` helper as **₦45,700**. Every figure on that tab — total, automated, manual, and the per-plan-tier breakdown — had the same defect. It was latent only for as long as every paying store was Nigerian; the platform already has Ghana/Kenya stores and a Cameroon (CFA) client in the pipeline.
- **Fix.** Added `totals_by_currency`, `automated_by_currency` and `manual_by_currency` to the `getOverview()` payload, each built by the new `App\Support\CurrencyTotals::fromPairs()` helper, which groups by uppercased ISO code and never converts between currencies. **No FX table was introduced** — per the Phase 1 spec, a converted single total would mean owning stale-rate risk and a new config surface, so the admin UI shows one line per currency instead. The pre-existing scalar keys (`total_revenue`, `manual_revenue`, `automated_revenue`) are left in place so nothing breaks mid-phase; the frontend moves onto the per-currency keys in the same phase.
- **A blank currency buckets under NGN** rather than creating an empty-string group. Both `payment_transactions.currency` and `stores.currency` are `NOT NULL` (the latter defaults to `'NGN'`, per `2026_05_16_000000_enhance_stores_table`), so a true null is unreachable from either table and the helper's null branch guards only future callers and blank strings. The spec drafted before this work assumed legacy rows carried `currency = NULL`; that premise was wrong, and the tests assert the reachable blank-string case instead.
- **Regression coverage.** `tests/Unit/Support/CurrencyTotalsTest.php` (5 cases: summing, descending sort, case normalisation, blank/null fallback, empty input) and `tests/Feature/Admin/AdminRevenueCurrencyBreakdownTest.php` (4 cases against real SQLite-backed rows: per-currency totals, the manual/automated split per currency, the blank-currency fallback, and empty breakdowns when no payments exist). RED first on all four feature cases (`Undefined array key "totals_by_currency"`); GREEN after. `AdminRevenueOverviewTest` still passes unchanged.
- **Deliberately not fixed here:** `getOverview()` still calls `->get()` on every matching transaction and paginates the result in PHP, violating `.agents/AGENTS.md` §8. Logged in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md` for Phase 3, which restructures that query anyway.

### A-167 — `permission_denied` could not tell "another tenant's store" from "my owner's other store", so a staff session draining the queue silently and permanently destroyed rows the owner could have pushed
- **Found:** logged out of A-165, escalated by A-166. A-161 made a `permission_denied` push refusal terminal *and* settling (`REASONS_SETTLING_SOURCE_ROW` in `client/lib/db/sync-engine/push.ts`): the queue row is dropped and the local row is flagged `_synced = 1`, after which no pull and no `requeueOrphanedRows()` pass can ever recover it. That was justified on "the caller has no access to this row's store, so no pull can settle it either", verified true for the five machine-seeded `permission_groups` rows A-161 actually fixed. A-165 then showed the sibling reason `forbidden` is *not* absolute — `resolveOwnershipIdentity()` gives a staff session only `[$currentUser->store_id]` where an owner gets every store they own — and A-166 routed four tables of real transactional data (`stock_audits`, `held_transactions`, `loyalty_transactions`, `customer_payments`) through the same guard. A-167 asked whether `permission_denied` had the same hole.
- **It did, and the enumeration is the evidence.** Three sites throw `SyncPushPermissionDeniedException`, and **none** of them was absolute:
  1. `normalizePushPayload()`'s explicit-`store_id` guard — `!in_array($payload['store_id'], $allowedStoreIds, true)`. `$allowedStoreIds` comes from `resolveAllowedOwnershipScope()` → `resolveOwnershipIdentity()`, the exact function A-165 showed narrows for staff. **Session-relative whenever the named store is another live store of the same owner.**
  2. and 3. `sanitizePermissionGroupSyncPayload()`'s "attempted to grant a permission the caller does not hold" and "caller lacks `manage_roles_permissions`". Both sit *below* an early `return $payload` for a `store_owner`/`admin`/`super_admin` caller, so every throw from either is by a non-owner session and the owner's session always passes. **Session-relative by construction, and they don't even need a second store.**
- **The reproduction, built rather than theorised** (`tests/Feature/SyncPushSessionScopedRefusalTest.php`). Owner owns Store A and Store B; staff user has `users.store_id = A` and owns no store. Staff pushes a `customer_payments` INSERT whose frozen payload names Store B — the shape A-165 established is reachable in current code, because `_sync_queue` is device-global and carries no identity and `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` only wipes when the *selected* store differs from the local one, so a staff login onto the owner's currently-active store drains the owner's other stores' queued rows under the narrower scope. Before the fix that came back `permission_denied`; the companion test then pushes the byte-identical payload as the owner and it is **accepted**, which is the whole bug in two assertions. A recorded customer payment was being dropped and settled on a refusal that was only ever true for the session that happened to be signed in.
- **Fix, server-side only, and why that shape.** The client cannot tell the two cases apart — it has no view of who owns which store — but the server can, so the distinction belongs in the reason string rather than in a client-side table allowlist. `normalizePushPayload()`'s guard now asks `storeBelongsToTenantOwner()` (is this one of the *tenant owner's* live stores?) before choosing what to throw: yes → the new `SyncPushForbiddenException`, reported as `forbidden`, which the client already treats as retryable and does **not** settle; no → the existing `SyncPushPermissionDeniedException` and the unchanged terminal-and-settle path. Both now extend a shared `SyncPushRefusalException` carrying a `reason()`, so `push()`'s per-change catch maps any structured refusal to its stable reason instead of special-casing one class and falling back to free text (the A-161 defect) for anything else.
  - **Why not blanket-revert A-161's settle:** that reopens the original bug — the five machine-seeded `permission_groups` rows retrying forever on a crash-looping device. Nothing here touches `REASONS_SETTLING_SOURCE_ROW`; `permission_denied` keeps exactly the treatment A-161 gave it. What changed is that it is now only *emitted* when it is true.
  - **Why not gate the settle on the table instead** (back to `TERMINAL_CONFLICT_SETTLES_SOURCE_ROW`'s shape): the hole is not a property of the table, it is a property of the session, so a table list would be wrong in both directions — it would still destroy a `permission_groups` row an owner really authored, and it would still park a `customer_payments` row for a genuinely foreign store that can never settle.
  - **Soft-deleted stores are deliberately on the absolute side.** `storeBelongsToTenantOwner()` uses the default (non-trashed) `Store` scope, exactly as `resolveOwnershipIdentity()` does, so a row queued for a store the owner has since deleted stays `permission_denied` and terminal — no session can ever accept it. This also matters for A-162, whose strongest remaining candidate is precisely a soft-deleted third store: if that is what happened to those five rows, this fix leaves A-161's behaviour for them untouched.
  - **An owner session's behaviour is unchanged by construction.** For an owner, `$allowedStoreIds` already *is* the owner's full live store set, so the new branch can never be true and every refusal an owner sees is still `permission_denied`. Pinned by its own test.
- **Deliberately not fixed here:** sites 2 and 3, the `permission_groups` privilege checks. One table, role *configuration* rather than transactional data, the local row survives, and the terminal classification is load-bearing in the other direction — a staff device whose store the server has already seeded would refuse its own client-seeded default groups, so making that retryable recreates A-161's stuck-item loop for every staff device. Logged as **A-168** in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md` with the reasoning, and pinned meanwhile by a decision-lock test so the current behaviour is a choice rather than an accident.
- **Regression coverage.** New file `tests/Feature/SyncPushSessionScopedRefusalTest.php`, seven cases: a staff session's INSERT and UPDATE naming the owner's other store are both refused `forbidden` with nothing written; the owner pushes the identical payload successfully; a genuinely foreign tenant's store is still `permission_denied` under both a staff and an owner session; a soft-deleted store of the same owner is still `permission_denied`; and the `permission_groups` privilege refusal is still `permission_denied`. RED first on exactly the two session-relative cases (`-'forbidden' +'permission_denied'`), the other five passing as the regression guards they are; GREEN after. No client change, so `client` was not re-run — `client/__tests__/push-settles-permission-denied-rows.test.ts` already pins both classifications and still holds. Full `laravel-server` suite: **915 passed, 2 skipped** (2847 assertions), up from 908/2.
- **Docs touched:** `client/AGENTS.md`'s push-reason paragraph now records that `permission_denied` is genuinely absolute (and that `forbidden` must still not be added to either set); A-166's "known behaviour change" bullet above is corrected, since the trade it took knowingly no longer has to be taken.

### A-166 — the four transactional store-scoped tables (`stock_audits`, `held_transactions`, `loyalty_transactions`, `customer_payments`) were also missing from `STORE_ID_BACKFILL_TABLES`, which both hid an unscoped INSERT from `pull()` forever and let an UPDATE hand the row to another tenant
- **Found:** logged out of the A-164 fix, confirmed here per table rather than by copying A-164's conclusion. All four carry a nullable `store_id` added by `2026_09_05_000002_fix_sync_schema_drift.php`, all four are in `resolveChangeStoreId()`'s `$directStoreTables`, in `client/lib/db/schema-migrations.ts`'s `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` and in `getModelForTable()`, and none of the four was in the write-side backfill list.
- **What the per-table investigation actually found, since A-166 was logged as "not a blind copy-paste".** The client's four creation paths — `lib/db/queries/inventory.ts`'s stock-audit reconciliation loop, `lib/hooks/use-pos-held-transactions.ts`, `lib/hooks/use-pos-payment-helpers.ts` + `lib/hooks/use-process-return-mutation.ts` (loyalty), and `lib/db/queries/customers.ts`'s `recordCustomerPayment()` — **none** of them names `store_id` in the object handed to `insert()`. They don't need to: `lib/db/base-helpers.ts`'s `insert()` auto-injects `store_id: getActiveStoreId()` for every table in `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES`, and the `_sync_queue` payload is that same post-injection record. So in the ordinary case the client *does* send a `store_id` and the backfill is redundant — but two paths still omit it, both real: (1) `getActiveStoreId()` is a module-level global set from `store-context.tsx`/`auth-context.tsx`, and the injection is guarded by a plain `storeId &&`, so any write that lands before it's set silently produces a `store_id`-less payload; (2) far more concretely, a `_sync_queue` payload is a **frozen snapshot**, and these four tables only joined `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` (and only gained a local `store_id` column) in the same 2026-09-05 wave. `backfillStoreIdOnLegacyRows()` repairs the *table* on every launch but never rewrites queue payloads, and for `held_transactions`/`loyalty_transactions`/`customer_payments` every push before that wave was silently dropped server-side for want of a `getModelForTable()` mapping — which is exactly why the migration had nothing to backfill for them and wrote that they "never had a working insert path at all". Those rows were therefore still sitting in `_sync_queue` with `store_id`-less payloads when the mapping went live, i.e. the symptom's most likely population is precisely the three tables the migration skipped.
- **The second, worse half, which the A-166 writeup had not predicted.** Membership in `STORE_ID_BACKFILL_TABLES` is also what refuses a payload that *names* a foreign `store_id`. Without it, an UPDATE for one of these four naming another tenant's store was **accepted**: `authorizeChangeTarget()` resolves ownership from the already-loaded row (ours, so allowed) and `forceFill($payload)` then applied the foreign `store_id` and saved it, moving a real stock audit or customer payment into a stranger's tenant. Verified before the fix by a probe run of the new cross-tenant UPDATE test with only its reason assertion removed: all four rows came back owned by the foreign store. The INSERT direction was already refused, but as `forbidden` from `authorizeInsertTarget()`.
- **Fix.** Added `stock_audits`, `held_transactions`, `loyalty_transactions` and `customer_payments` to `SyncController::STORE_ID_BACKFILL_TABLES` — the whole fix, deliberately, because every behaviour above follows from that one list and A-164 had already made the list operation-aware. Nothing else was touched: the INSERT-only gate in `normalizePushPayload()` and the matching "drop a merely-backfilled `store_id` when `push()` rewrites an INSERT into an UPDATE" branch in `push()` are both keyed off this same constant, so the four tables inherit A-164's protection against UPDATE-reassignment automatically rather than needing a parallel code path — and the new tests pin that, so a future change that reintroduces an unconditional backfill fails for these four as well as for the loyalty tables.
- **One correction to the A-166 finding as logged:** it said all four were excluded from the 2026-09-05 migration's one-off backfill. Only three were. `stock_audits` *is* in that migration's backfill loop (alongside `audit_logs`, `loyalty_tiers`, `loyalty_redemption_options`) because it already had a working insert path; the three genuinely excluded ones are `held_transactions`, `loyalty_transactions`, `customer_payments`. That makes no difference to the fix — `stock_audits` was equally missing from the *write-side* list, which is what this entry is about — but the distinction is why its pre-existing rows were already repaired server-side and the other three's never needed to be.
- **Known behaviour change, and the open item it feeds.** A cross-tenant INSERT for these four is now refused `permission_denied` instead of `forbidden`, the same side effect A-164 noted for the loyalty tables. That is the stricter and more informative reason, but the client treats `permission_denied` as terminal *and* settles the source row (A-161), so under the staff-session narrowing described in A-165/A-167 a legitimate row for the owner's *other* store would now be dropped rather than parked — and unlike A-164's seeded loyalty config, these four hold real transactional data. The trade was taken knowingly: the alternative is leaving the cross-tenant UPDATE reassignment above open, which is strictly worse. **That trade no longer has to be taken: A-167 (below) confirmed the hole was real and closed it the same day**, so a refusal naming another *live* store of the same tenant owner now comes back `forbidden` (parked, recoverable) and only a genuinely foreign or gone store comes back `permission_denied`. The sentence above is kept as written because it records why the trade looked acceptable at the time; it no longer describes current behaviour.
- **No data sweep was run.** The A-166 note asked for a server-side sweep for pre-existing `store_id IS NULL` rows in these four tables. That is a production-database action and this work was kept local per the standing no-live-production-testing rule; it stays a separate, explicitly-requested operational step. The code fix stops new ones from being created either way.
- **Regression coverage.** New file `tests/Feature/SyncPushStoreIdBackfillTransactionalTest.php` (`SyncPushStoreIdBackfillTest.php` was already 276 lines, so appending 20 cases would have broken the 350-line rule), five data-provider-driven cases × four tables = 20 tests: an INSERT with no `store_id` lands scoped to the pusher's store; an INSERT naming a foreign store is refused `permission_denied` and never written; an UPDATE omitting `store_id`, pushed with `X-Store-Id` naming a second store the same owner owns, still applies the edit but leaves `store_id` alone; the same for an INSERT that `push()` rewrites into an UPDATE; and an UPDATE naming a foreign `store_id` is refused with the row unchanged. RED first: 12 of the 20 failed for the right reasons (4 × `store_id` NULL, 4 × `forbidden` instead of `permission_denied`, 4 × the row actually reassigned to the foreign store), the other 8 passing as the regression guards they are. GREEN after the one-line list change. Full suite: 908 passed, 2 skipped.

### A-164 — `loyalty_tiers`/`loyalty_redemption_options` were missing from `normalizePushPayload()`'s `store_id` backfill list, so a pushed tier or redemption option with no `store_id` was written unscoped and never pulled back down
- **Found:** logged during the A-163 investigation, confirmed here by diffing `SyncController::normalizePushPayload()`'s `$tablesWithStoreId` (`app/Http/Controllers/Api/App/SyncController.php`) against `resolveChangeStoreId()`'s `$directStoreTables` in the same file and against `client/lib/db/schema-migrations.ts`'s `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES`. Both loyalty-config tables appear in the latter two lists and in `getModelForTable()`, but not in the backfill list.
- **Root cause.** The two tables were created without a `store_id` at all (`2026_07_25_000002_create_loyalty_settings_tables.php`); the column was added later, by the schema-drift sweep in `2026_09_05_000002_fix_sync_schema_drift.php`, which also one-off-backfilled the existing rows. That sweep updated the read/authorization side (`$directStoreTables`, `pull()`'s per-table store scoping) but not the write-side backfill list. The two store-scoped tables added to `$tablesWithStoreId` *after* that date were each added reactively for their own incident — `audit_logs` in `e4e4c235` ("audit_logs store_id never backfilled on sync push") and `permission_groups` in `a5f40463` — so these two were simply never revisited. Consequence: a client INSERT that omitted `store_id` skipped the `if (empty($payload['store_id'])) { $payload['store_id'] = $currentStoreId; }` branch entirely, `resolveChangeStoreId()` then returned `null`, `authorizeInsertTarget()` returned `true` on that null (its deliberate "can't determine ⇒ not denied" fallback), and the row was written with `store_id IS NULL` — accepted, never retried, and permanently invisible to `pull()`'s `whereIn('store_id', $storeIds)` for that table, so the tier or redemption option never came back down to any device. Not a tenancy hole: the *other* branch of the same `if` already refused a payload naming a foreign store.
- **Fix, part 1.** Added `loyalty_tiers` and `loyalty_redemption_options` to the backfill list (now the class constant `STORE_ID_BACKFILL_TABLES`, promoted from the local `$tablesWithStoreId` so `push()` can read it too — see part 2). The backfill, the foreign-store refusal and the `pull()` visibility all follow from membership in that one list. A side effect worth noting: a cross-tenant INSERT for either table is now refused by that list's own guard as `permission_denied` (`SyncPushPermissionDeniedException`, per A-161) instead of falling through to `authorizeInsertTarget()`'s `forbidden`, which is strictly better while `forbidden` is still absent from the client's `NON_RETRYABLE_CONFLICT_REASONS` (A-165).
- **Fix, part 2 — the backfill had to be operation-gated, found by review before this shipped.** `normalizePushPayload()` runs unconditionally for INSERT, UPDATE *and* DELETE, and its `if (empty($payload['store_id'])) { $payload['store_id'] = $currentStoreId; }` branch never checked which. Client update payloads for these tables (and for `products`, via `use-save-product-mutation.ts` / `use-product-quick-edit-mutation.ts`) never send `store_id` at all, because store ownership is treated as immutable once a row exists. So an UPDATE pushed while a *different* store was the request's active store — the multi-store-owner race already documented in A-162/A-163, where queued writes from the previous store flush after the switch — had `store_id` backfilled to the **wrong** store, and `$model->forceFill($payload)` then applied it onto the already-loaded row before `save()`, silently reassigning that row to a store it never belonged to. Nothing upstream caught it: `authorizeChangeTarget()` runs *before* the `forceFill` and resolves ownership from the loaded model's real `store_id` via `resolveChangeStoreId()`, not from the payload, so a second store the same owner legitimately owns passes authorization cleanly. The backfill is now gated to `INSERT` only; on UPDATE/DELETE an empty `store_id` key is dropped from the payload outright, so `forceFill()` can neither reassign the column nor null it out. The *other* branch of that same `if` — rejecting an **explicitly** named foreign `store_id` — stays unconditional: it is a security check, not a backfill, and a client naming another tenant's store in an update payload must still be refused.
- **Why `$change['operation']` alone wasn't a sufficient gate.** The "an INSERT for an id that already exists is rewritten to an UPDATE" branch in `push()` runs *after* the `normalizePushPayload()` call, so at normalization time `$change['operation']` is only the client's original intent, not the operation actually executed. A re-queued INSERT for an existing row (`requeueOrphanedRows()` produces exactly these, and any retried push does too) therefore still passed the `INSERT` gate and still carried a backfilled `store_id` into the UPDATE path. Rather than reorder the pipeline — `sanitizePermissionGroupSyncPayload()` also takes `$change['operation']`, so moving the existence probe above the normalization would change its behaviour too — `push()` now records whether the client itself named a `store_id` before normalizing, and drops a merely-backfilled one at the point it rewrites the operation to `UPDATE`. Both halves are load-bearing; each was verified to fail its own test without the other.
- **Known behaviour change.** A legacy row whose stored `store_id` is `NULL` no longer gets it stamped as a side effect of any UPDATE/DELETE push. The client's own `backfillStoreIdOnLegacyRows` sets `store_id` locally and sends it explicitly, which still works (and is still validated against the caller's stores), and the DELETE path keeps its separate explicit legacy-claim branch; only the implicit, active-store-derived stamping is gone, which is the behaviour that could not tell a legacy row from a row belonging to another of the owner's stores.
- **Regression coverage.** `tests/Feature/SyncPushStoreIdBackfillTest.php` (kept separate rather than appended to `SyncPushOwnershipTest.php`, already 766 lines), six cases: a `loyalty_tiers` INSERT and a `loyalty_redemption_options` INSERT, each with no `store_id` in the payload, must come back with zero failures and land scoped to the pusher's store (both `null` before part 1); an INSERT naming another owner's `store_id` is refused and never written; an UPDATE omitting `store_id`, pushed with `X-Store-Id` naming a *second store the same owner owns*, must leave the row's `store_id` untouched while still applying the rest of the payload; the same for an INSERT that `push()` rewrites into an UPDATE; and an UPDATE explicitly naming a foreign `store_id` is still refused `permission_denied` with the row unchanged. Full suite: 888 passed, 2 skipped.
- **Deliberately not fixed here, and since closed:** the same diff found four more tables missing from that list (`stock_audits`, `held_transactions`, `loyalty_transactions`, `customer_payments`), each needing its own check of what the client actually sends rather than a blanket list addition. Logged as A-166, investigated per table and fixed the same day — see the A-166 entry above, which also corrects this one's claim that all four were excluded from the 2026-09-05 migration's backfill (`stock_audits` was not).

### A-163 — `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` left three store-scoped tables behind, so an account/store switch orphaned rows that `requeueOrphanedRows()` then pushed under the new identity forever (the A-162 mechanism)
- **Found:** tracing A-162 (why one device queued five `permission_groups` rows for a store its session cannot write). `LOCAL_WIPE_TABLES` (`client/lib/db/core.ts`) is shared by `resetDatabase()` and `clearDatabaseForNewStore()`, and comparing it against `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` (`client/lib/db/schema-migrations.ts`) showed exactly three store-scoped tables missing from it: **`permission_groups`, `loyalty_tiers`, `loyalty_redemption_options`**. The omission is correct for `resetDatabase()` — its `stores` row survives, both seeders are gated on that row's `permission_groups_seeded_at`/loyalty stamps, so wiping the config there would leave staff with dangling `users.permission_group_id` and no reseed — but it is wrong for `clearDatabaseForNewStore()`, which deletes `stores`/`users` precisely because the store is being replaced.
- **Root cause.** `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` runs on an account/store switch (`app/setup/use-onboarding.ts`'s `confirmCloudRestoreSwitch`, reached whenever the local store differs from the one being linked) and clears `_sync_queue` along with the data tables. The previous store's `permission_groups` rows were not in its list, so they survived the wipe at `_synced = 0` **with their queue entry deleted** — which is exactly the shape `requeueOrphanedRows()` (`lib/db/reconcile-identity.ts`) exists to repair. `DatabaseProvider` hands it `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES`, which *does* include `permission_groups`, once per install and on every crash-flagged launch (`force: hadPendingCrashes`). It re-queues each orphan as an `INSERT` preserving the row's original `created_at`, so the rows are pushed under the **new** account's bearer token carrying the **old** store's `store_id`, and `SyncController::normalizePushPayload()` refuses them as cross-tenant — permanently, since resending cannot change who owns that store. Five rows, one per default role, is the exact signature A-161 saw. The same orphan hole existed for the two loyalty-config tables; they simply produced no Sentry noise on this device (nothing had seeded unsynced loyalty rows there), and they would have been refused one step earlier by `authorizeInsertTarget()` as `forbidden` rather than by this guard. Two lesser gaps found alongside and logged in `KNOWN_BUGS.md` rather than patched here: how those two tables' `store_id` is backfilled (A-164), and the fact that `forbidden` is still absent from the client's `NON_RETRYABLE_CONFLICT_REASONS` (A-165) — the same permanent-retry class A-161 just fixed for `permission_denied`.
- **Fix.** `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` now derives its list as `LOCAL_WIPE_TABLES` + every `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` entry missing from it + `stores`/`users`, instead of hardcoding the two extras. `resetDatabase()` is untouched and still keeps store configuration. Deriving it from `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` rather than naming the three tables means a future store-scoped table cannot reintroduce this class — the same reasoning as the shared-constant fix that stopped `resetDatabase()`/`clearDatabaseForNewStore()` drifting over `sale_item_batches`.
- **What this does and does not claim.** It closes the only path by which a store-scoped row can outlive the wipe meant to erase it and then be re-queued under a different identity, which is the most likely mechanism behind A-162's five rows and a real defect on its own evidence. It does not establish *which* store id those five frozen payloads actually carry — that is still open as a narrowed A-162 in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md`, together with what this investigation newly ruled out.
- **Dating evidence recovered while tracing this** (corrects A-161's note above): the five record ids are random because `982e8e4b` — the commit that *introduced* seeding, 2026-09-27 — used `generateId()`; `deterministicDefaultGroupId()` arrived later the same day in `a5f40463`. So the rows were seeded inside that one-day window on **2026-09-27**, not "a client release before 982e8e4b" (before it there was no seeding at all), and A-161's deterministic-id check could not have matched by construction. Running that function over both of this owner's real stores (`571582a9…`, and `68e4c0e5…` created 2026-10-05) matches none of the five ids, as expected for a `generateId()` build. The refusing guard itself dates to `132dee87` (2026-09-19), so these rows were refused on their very first push attempt — no regression window, nothing ever accepted them.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/clear-database-for-new-store-clears-store-scoped-tables.test.ts` — seeds one row into every `STORE_SCOPED_TABLES` table that has a `store_id` column (driven off `PRAGMA table_info`, so it needs no per-table maintenance) and asserts none survives the wipe, plus an explicit case for the five default `permission_groups`. Confirmed RED listing exactly `loyalty_tiers`, `loyalty_redemption_options`, `permission_groups` as survivors, GREEN after. Full client suite: 2410 passed across 381 files. No server-side change, so `laravel-server` was not re-run.

### A-161 — a cross-tenant `store_id` sync refusal was reported as free text instead of `permission_denied`, so five `permission_groups` rows retried forever on one device (Sentry DUMOSRX-CLIENT-1V)
- **Found:** Sentry `DUMOSRX-CLIENT-1V`, `Error: Sync item stuck after 5 attempts on permission_groups/<uuid>: Sync push: store_id in payload is outside the caller's allowed stores`. One device (`DRX-OCGGTOPX2`, iPhone / Mobile Safari, `store_id` tag `571582a9-bc11-43e2-81e9-57999db50294` — Cynthia's store). Listing the issue's individual events showed **exactly five distinct `permission_groups` record ids**, all reported in the same second: the five default groups (Admin/Manager/Specialist/Sales Staff/Auditor). Their ids are random, not the `deterministicDefaultGroupId(storeId, role)` values for the tagged store (checked by running that function over all five roles), which dates them to a client release before 982e8e4b's deterministic-id change — i.e. these rows had been sitting in that device's `_sync_queue` since roughly late September. A broad search for the error message across the project found **no other issue, table, device or store**: the queued rows are the only ones that ever hit it.
- **Root cause.** `SyncController::normalizePushPayload()`'s guard for an INSERT payload naming a `store_id` outside `$allowedStoreIds` threw a bare `\RuntimeException`. `push()`'s per-change catch maps only `SyncPushPermissionDeniedException` to the stable reason `permission_denied` and otherwise falls back to `$e->getMessage()` — so this refusal came back to the client as the free-text sentence above. `client/lib/db/sync-engine/push.ts` matches the reason against `NON_RETRYABLE_CONFLICT_REASONS` to decide whether a rejection is terminal; a free-text reason matches nothing, so each row went through `recordSyncFailure()`'s exponential backoff instead, re-queued at the 1-hour cap forever, and re-raised `Sync item stuck after 5 attempts` from the Sentry reporter. The refusal can never succeed on a retry — the queued payload is frozen and the caller's store ownership does not change by resending — so this is the same "privilege rejection is terminal" class the two `sanitizePermissionGroupSyncPayload()` checks on this very same code path already got right, and the identical condition a few lines later in `push()` (`authorizeInsertTarget()`) already reports as a structured `forbidden`. It was the only authorization refusal on the push path still classified as a generic error.
- **Fix (two halves, both needed).** (1) Server: that guard now throws `SyncPushPermissionDeniedException`, so the reason is `permission_denied` — already in both `NON_RETRYABLE_CONFLICT_REASONS` (queue row dropped on the first response, with a `recordTerminalConflict()` ledger row as the durable trace) and `SILENT_TERMINAL_REASONS` (no toast; nobody asked for this write). (2) Client: a `permission_denied` drop now also settles the source row via the new `REASONS_SETTLING_SOURCE_ROW`, alongside the existing per-table `TERMINAL_CONFLICT_SETTLES_SOURCE_ROW`. Without that second half the fix would not hold: dropping only the queue row leaves the local row at `_synced = 0` with no queue entry, which is exactly what `requeueOrphanedRows()` re-queues — once per install normally, but on *every* crash-flagged launch (`DatabaseProvider`'s `force: hadPendingCrashes`), and this device crashes constantly (it is the same device as A-159/A-160). Keyed on the reason rather than the table because a caller with no access to that store cannot pull the row either, so no pull on any table can settle it; `version_conflict` deliberately still leaves the row unsettled, since there the server does have it and the next pull is the reconciler.
- **Nothing of value is dropped here.** These are machine-generated default groups whose permission lists come from a hardcoded client constant, and the server lazily seeds its own copy for every store anyway (`PermissionGroupSeeder::ensureSeeded()`, called from `validateSync()` on every push and pull). The security check itself is untouched — the write is still refused.
- **Deliberately not fixed, and why:** *why* that device queued rows for a store its session cannot write to is not determinable from the available evidence — logged as A-162 in `docs/KNOWN_BUGS.md`. This entry fixes the permanent-retry/alert-noise defect, which is independently real and independently verifiable; it does not claim to explain the store mismatch.
- **Regression coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPushOwnershipTest.php::test_insert_with_store_id_outside_callers_stores_is_reported_as_permission_denied` (asserts `failed.0.reason === 'permission_denied'` for a `permission_groups` INSERT naming another tenant's store, and that nothing is written) — confirmed RED returning the exact production sentence, GREEN after. `client/__tests__/push-settles-permission-denied-rows.test.ts` (a `permission_denied` drop clears the queue row *and* flags the source row synced, and a following `requeueOrphanedRows()` pass cannot resurrect it; a `version_conflict` on the same table still leaves it unsynced) — confirmed RED on the settle assertion, GREEN after. Full suites: `laravel-server` 882 passed / 2 skipped (2741 assertions), `client` 2408 passed across 380 files.

### A-160 — the service worker's navigate timeout was too short for a congested connection to ever land a fresh page, so that device fell back to (stale) cache forever (Sentry DUMOSRX-CLIENT-F + DUMOSRX-CLIENT-J)
- **Found:** following up on A-159 — Sentry showed `DUMOSRX-CLIENT-F` (`SyntaxError: Unexpected token '<'`) and `DUMOSRX-CLIENT-J` (`TypeError: Failed to fetch`) firing together, repeatedly, for the same single device on a slow/congested Nigerian connection. A-159 fixed the *caching* half of the loop (`cache: "no-store"` on the navigate fetch, `Cache-Control: no-cache` for `.html`), but the paired `Failed to fetch` showed the other half: that device's network couldn't complete the navigation fetch inside the handler's budget at all, so no fix to cache *freshness* could help it — it never got a fresh response to store in the first place.
- **Root cause.** `client/public/sw.js`'s navigate branch aborted its "network first" fetch after a flat `4000ms`. That number was chosen for one reason only, documented in place: iOS Safari parks a doomed fetch on a dead connection for tens of seconds instead of rejecting, so without an abort the user watches a spinner for that whole window before the offline fallback runs. It was never sized against a *live but slow* connection. On a congested mobile link, 4s regularly expires before the document's first byte arrives, so every navigation aborted (logged as `Failed to fetch`), fell through to the `cache.match()` fallback, and served whatever Cache Storage held — which on this device was a stale entry referencing chunks from a deploy that no longer exists, served back by the static host's catch-all `index.html` as HTML and parsed as JS (`Unexpected token '<'`). Self-sustaining: the fallback path can never replace the stale entry, because only the fetch it keeps abandoning writes to the cache.
- **Fix.** The budget is now a named `NAVIGATE_NETWORK_TIMEOUT_MS = 10000` constant. 10s is the smallest value with real headroom over worst-case time-to-first-byte on a congested 3G-class mobile link (a few seconds of DNS/TLS/queueing plus server time, where 4s is routinely exceeded and still represents a request that would have succeeded), while staying well inside the tens-of-seconds iOS hang the abort exists to cut — the user sees at most 10s before the offline fallback, not a blank page.
- **Shape deliberately left alone.** A "first byte vs. total request time" split was considered and is unnecessary: `fetch()` resolves as soon as response *headers* arrive, and the timer is cleared in that same `.finally()`, so this budget already only bounds time-to-headers — the document body streams on afterwards unbounded, with no abort left armed. A single constant is therefore the correct scope; this is now noted in the comment above the `AbortController` so it isn't re-litigated.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/sw-navigate-slow-network-timeout.test.ts` — evaluates `sw.js` against a stub `self` (same harness as `sw-cache-retention.test.ts`) and drives the real `fetch` listener with a never-resolving navigation request on fake timers: asserts the exposed constant sits in the 8–12s band, that nothing has aborted or fallen back at 7s (the case the old 4s value broke), and that the abort still fires at the budget and resolves to the cached document. Confirmed RED on all three cases before the fix, GREEN after; full suite (2406 tests) passes.

### A-159 — one real device got permanently stuck on a single stale deploy, unable to reach Inventory > Catalog ("Unexpected token '<'", Sentry DUMOSRX-CLIENT-F)
- **Found:** live, reported by the user from Cynthia's store, plus a DevTools screenshot showing `SyntaxError: Unexpected token '<'` repeating on `app.dumosrx.com/inventory`. Sentry confirmed: `DUMOSRX-CLIENT-F`, 228 events / 4 users over 30 days, but **178 of those 228 (78%) were one single device (`DRX-8DY6G5XH8`, Cynthia's store, `store_id` `571582a9-bc11-43e2-81e9-57999db50294`), all pinned to one single old release (`3209388c...`)** — every other device/release combination in the same issue had 1–14 events, consistent with this app's existing stale-chunk auto-recovery working as designed for everyone else.
- **Root cause.** This is a static export (`output: export`) host that falls back to `index.html` (200 OK, HTML) for any path it doesn't recognize rather than a real 404 — so a stale tab's lazy-loaded chunk request "succeeds" with an HTML document the browser then tries to execute as JS, throwing exactly `SyntaxError: Unexpected token '<'` (the start of `<!DOCTYPE html>`). The app already has real engineering for this (`lib/utils/chunk-error.ts`, `global-error-listener.tsx`, `error-boundary.tsx`): it auto-reloads the tab once, guarded by a `sessionStorage` flag so it can't loop forever. That guard is the gap — it survives a same-tab reload (only clears on tab close, or on `GlobalErrorListener` observing a clean boot) — so if the reload lands on the *same* stale content again, the one-shot recovery is already spent and the device is stuck until the tab fully closes. Two real gaps let that happen: (1) `public/sw.js`'s navigate handler is documented as "network first" but its own `fetch(request, ...)` call carried no `cache` option, unlike `fetchManifest()` a few lines above it which deliberately passes `cache: "no-store"` for this exact reason — so even the service worker's "network first" fetch could be quietly satisfied by the browser's own HTTP cache. (2) `public/.htaccess` set no `Cache-Control` at all for HTML documents, leaving them to the browser's heuristic caching with no explicit freshness rule, independent of the service worker entirely.
- **Fix.** `public/sw.js`'s navigate fetch now passes `cache: "no-store"` alongside its existing abort signal. `public/.htaccess` gains a `mod_headers` block setting `Cache-Control: no-cache, no-store, must-revalidate` on `*.html`, leaving `_next/static/*` (content-hashed, safe to cache indefinitely) untouched.
- **Immediate unblock for Cynthia** (not a code fix, just what gets her working again before this ships): a hard refresh (Ctrl+Shift+R) or clearing site data for `app.dumosrx.com` forces a real network fetch past both the stale service-worker cache and the stale HTTP cache.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/sw-navigate-no-store.test.ts` (asserts the navigate branch's own `fetch(request, ...)` call carries `cache: "no-store"`, distinct from the runtime stale-while-revalidate branch's plain `fetch(request)` for immutable hashed assets, which deliberately keeps none); `client/__tests__/htaccess-no-cache-html.test.ts` (asserts `.htaccess` sets a no-cache `Cache-Control` header for `.html`). Both confirmed RED before their fix, GREEN after; full suite (2403 tests) passes.

### A-158 — a cluster of UI bugs found from phone photos of a real store's older Windows machine, most tracing back to one shared root cause
- **Found:** live, by the user, from phone photos of a real store's older Windows machine (`refs/client-requirement-meeting-with-ada-progressive/6thOct/*.HEIC`, converted to `.jpg` for review) — the dashboard's Action Center cards had no visible background, the AI assistant launcher looked like "a circle button on top of a square button," the Inventory tab bar's and a Purchase Orders filter chip's hover state made their label text disappear entirely, the Settings sidebar had the same disappearing-text hover issue, and the Report Center's report cards wrapped their View/Export/Print buttons onto a second line.
- **Root cause (shared across the hover/background cases).** `app/globals.css`'s `@supports not (color: color-mix(in lab, red, red))` block exists specifically to stop a theme-CSS-variable opacity utility (e.g. `bg-destructive/10`) from silently falling back to a SOLID, full-opacity color on a browser that can't parse `color-mix()` (e.g. Chrome <111 / Windows 7, the class of machine these photos were taken on) — which would otherwise collide with same-colored foreground text/icons and make them invisible. Each case below is a different way a component's styling slipped past that safety net, or past having an opaque surface in the first place.
  1. **Action Center cards** (`components/dashboard/dashboard-action-center.tsx`) passed `bg-destructive/10`-style tint classes straight into `<Card>`'s `className`, which replaced — not layered on top of — Card's own opaque `bg-card` background via Tailwind's class-merging. Already a faint, inconsistent tint on a modern browser; on the old one, the compat shim deliberately zeroes that same tint to literal `transparent` (its documented trade-off), leaving the card with no surface at all.
  2. **Tab/filter-chip hover text** (`components/ui/tabs.tsx`, and every filter-chip component built on it: `components/procurement/purchase-order-status-filter.tsx`, `components/procurement/requested-products-status-filter.tsx`, `components/stock-batch/supplier-status-filter.tsx`, `components/pos/pos-category-filter.tsx`): inactive-tab/chip hover uses the *compound* Tailwind variant `data-[state=inactive]:hover:bg-primary/10`, a different literal class string than the bare `hover:bg-primary/10` tokens the compat shim's selector list covered. It slipped past the shim entirely, so the old browser's un-mitigated behavior kicked in: the "10% tint" became solid, fully-opaque primary blue, and the hover text (`hover:text-primary`, a solid color, unaffected by the color-mix issue itself) rendered the same blue on top of it — invisible. The Settings sidebar (`app/(dashboard)/settings/[tab]/settings-tab-nav.tsx`) and the Purchase Orders status filter both use the same underlying `TabsTrigger`, so both showed the identical symptom from the one shared cause.
- **Unrelated causes, found alongside the above.**
  3. **AI assistant launcher** (`components/assistant/assistant-launcher.tsx`): its header wrapper (`components/dashboard/dashboard-header.tsx:122`) draws a `rounded-full` ring around it with only 2px of padding, but the button itself kept the `Button` component's default `rounded-md` corners with a persistently-visible gradient fill — the square corners visibly clashed with the circular ring. A plain shape mismatch, nothing to do with color-mix.
  4. **Report Center button wrap** (`components/reports/report-card.tsx`): the report icon sat in its own flex column spanning the card's full height, alongside title/description/buttons together — so the button row (View/Export/Print) only had the card's width *minus* the icon's column to work with, and Print wrapped to a second line on a narrower screen.
- **Fix.** (1) Card's tint moved to a separate absolutely-positioned overlay div on top of an explicit `bg-card` base, rather than inside Card's own background slot (`tintStyles`/`surfaceStyles` split, replacing the single `bgStyles` map). (2) Added the compound-variant selector `[class~="data-[state=inactive]:hover:bg-primary/10"][data-state="inactive"]:hover` to the compat shim — covers every file in the bullet above, since they all emit the identical literal class token. (3) Added `rounded-full` to both of the launcher's className variants. (4) Moved the icon into the title row (icon-title-tag as one row), so the description and button row below span the card's full width.
- **Also fixed in passing.** `refs/client-requirement-meeting-with-ada-progressive/6thOct/*.HEIC` (gitignored, not tracked) converted to `.jpg` at the user's request, for easier review going forward.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/action-center-card-opaque-background.test.tsx` (renders the real component, asserts the card keeps `bg-card`); `client/__tests__/tabs-hover-compat-shim-coverage.test.ts` (scans every `.tsx` file under `components/` and `app/` for this compound opacity-token pattern and asserts `globals.css` has a matching override for each — generic project-wide guard, not pinned to the files known at fix time); `client/__tests__/assistant-launcher-plan-gate.test.tsx` (new case asserting `rounded-full` on the rendered button); `client/__tests__/report-card-layout.test.tsx` (asserts the report icon renders in the title row, not spanning down into the button row). All confirmed RED before their fix, GREEN after; full suite (2399 tests, 1 pre-existing unrelated flaky teardown in `sync-engine.test.ts`) passes.

### A-157 — re-importing a category onto an existing, still-categoryless product (no barcode) silently created a duplicate product instead of updating it
- **Found:** investigating why a new store's (Cynthia/Agidi branch) xlsx import never picked up categories — the source file's QuickBooks POS export template had no department/category column at all, so the first import correctly left every product categoryless. The real bug surfaced while planning the *fix*: re-importing a corrected file with a category column added would have created duplicates for every one of those products instead of labelling them.
- **Root cause.** `findExistingProductId()` (`client/lib/db/queries/product-import.ts`) matched by barcode first, then — whenever the row had a category value — matched by name+category together with no further fallback. A barcode-less row whose name matched an existing product, but whose category didn't match that product's current category (including the common case of the existing product having `category_id IS NULL`), returned `null` outright instead of falling through to the plain by-name lookup used when the row has no category at all. `importProductRows()` then treated that `null` as "new product" and inserted a second row with the same name.
- **Fix.** Added one more fallback inside the `row.category` branch: if no row has the exact name+category yet, look for an existing row with the same name that is *still categoryless* (`category_id IS NULL`) and match that instead. A product that already has a *different* category is still treated as a deliberate distinct product (unchanged behavior, still covered by the existing "treats the same name in different categories as distinct products" test).
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/product-import-upsert.test.ts` — new case "updates an existing categoryless, barcode-less product when the re-import adds a category, instead of creating a duplicate". Confirmed RED (created a duplicate) before the fix, GREEN after; full existing suite in that file (10 cases) still passes, including the distinct-categories case.

## 2026-10-02

### A-156 — a brand-new signup's Pro trial was granted server-side immediately but didn't show up locally until the next background sync pull
- **Found:** live, by the user, immediately after A-154 shipped — reported not seeing the Pro trial right after signing up.
- **Root cause.** `RegistersAccounts::register()` creates the trial `Subscription` row server-side in the same request/transaction as the user+store (confirmed directly against the local dev database: an `active`, `is_trial=1`, `pro` subscription existed for the account, timestamped to the exact second of signup). But `app/setup/use-onboarding.ts`'s `finishNewAccountSetup()` inserts the local `stores` row without a `subscription_tier` value, so it took the SQLite schema's hardcoded default, `'free'` (`lib/db/schema.ts:340`). That only gets corrected to the real plan once a sync **pull** re-fetches the store row — `SyncController::mapPullRowForClient()` is what computes `subscription_tier`/`license_token` from the real subscription — but the post-signup `sync(false, true)` call is fire-and-forget (`.catch(console.error)`, never awaited before `router.push("/dashboard")`), so the dashboard could render before that pull ever completed. `Billing Settings` was unaffected, since it calls `GET /subscription/status` live rather than reading local SQLite.
- **Fix.** `finishNewAccountSetup()` now calls the existing `syncSubscriptionStatus()` (`lib/db/sync-engine/index.ts`) immediately after creating the store, instead of waiting on the later fire-and-forget `sync()` call. That helper already exists for exactly this: a privileged, bypass-free-tier-block pull of only the `stores` table's subscription-critical columns, applying the server's own `mapPullRowForClient()`-computed `subscription_tier`/`license_token` (including its `($plan !== 'free' || $isTrial) && !empty($expiry)` null-token guard) rather than reconstructing that shape by hand client-side — first draft of this fix did exactly that by hand, calling `apiClient.getSubscriptionStatus()` directly and building the license-token JSON itself, which `/code-review` flagged as liable to drift from the canonical shape; switched to reusing `syncSubscriptionStatus()` before shipping. Scoped to this one online, cloud-first signup path — the separate offline/no-network registration branch further down the same file has no connectivity to call the server with in the first place, so it's left relying on the existing background-sync correction once the device comes online, as designed.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/onboarding-seeds-subscription-status.test.ts` — asserts `syncSubscriptionStatus()` is called and awaited before `router.push("/dashboard")`, and that a failed status fetch doesn't block account creation. Confirmed RED by reverting the fix and re-running.

### A-154 — `/login`'s recent-user picker could still show a stale, already-wiped account after a fresh signup, even with A-152 fixed
- **Found:** live, by the user — after the A-152 fix shipped, they still reported seeing a stale account's tile on `/login` right after completing a brand-new signup; a follow-up question ("since when did we start showing recent users first?") led to re-opening the investigation via static code reading only (no live reproduction).
- **Root cause.** `/login`'s setup and login tabs are one mounted page instance for the whole register → confirm-wipe → new-account flow (`app/login/use-login-page.tsx`'s own design: "no navigation, no remount, no second loading spinner"). `hooks/use-device-auth-status.ts`'s `useDeviceAuthStatus()` read `userCount`/`recentUsers` **once**, in a `useEffect(() => {...}, [])`, on that instance's first mount — i.e. *before* `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` ever ran. If anything lands the user back on `/login` within that same component lifetime (the suspect: `components/dashboard/dashboard-layout.tsx`'s `if (!user) router.push("/login")`, since a search-param-only client navigation back to the same route segment doesn't remount it), the picker rendered from that pre-wipe snapshot, showing the old account even though the actual DB/localStorage were already correctly wiped and repopulated. Distinct from A-152: that was a storage-level leak (fixed); this is a stale in-memory React snapshot that survives A-152's fix untouched, since it's never re-read.
- **Fix.** `useDeviceAuthStatus()` now exposes a `refetch()` that re-runs the same `userCount`/`recentUsers` read (factored into a shared `readStatus()` helper so the mount effect and `refetch()` can't drift apart). `useOnboarding()` takes an optional `onDeviceDataChanged` callback and calls it after every point this device's local user data actually changes: right after `clearDatabaseForNewStore()`, and after each of the three `login()` call sites that can follow it (the two signup branches, plus `startSyncProcess`'s cloud-restore/link-existing-account `login(email)` — caught by an independent fable review and `/code-review` both flagging the same missed call site before this was considered done). `use-login-page.tsx` wires `useDeviceAuthStatus()`'s `refetch` into `useOnboarding(refetch)`. Tab switching still doesn't trigger a refetch, preserving the no-flash behavior the hook was designed for.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/onboarding-refetches-device-auth-status.test.ts` — asserts the injected callback fires exactly twice (after the wipe, after login) and in the correct order relative to both. Confirmed RED by reverting the `onDeviceDataChanged?.()` calls and re-running.

### A-152 — `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` wiped the `users`/`stores` tables but left a disassociated account's profile tile on the login screen
- **Found:** live, by the user, immediately after exercising the just-fixed A-150 flow — confirmed the account-switch wipe, linked a brand-new "pikadev" account, and the login screen's "Welcome Back / select your profile" picker still showed the old "Pika" account's tile.
- **Root cause.** The picker (`hooks/use-device-auth-status.ts`, rendered by `components/auth/user-selection.tsx`) reads its tiles from `localStorage`'s `dumos_recent_users` cache (`storage-keys.ts`'s `getRecentUsers()`), not from the `users` table. `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` wipes `users`/`stores` (and every other local table) but never touched this separate cache, so a disassociated account's tile — and the ability to click into it — survived the exact wipe meant to disconnect this device from it entirely.
- **Fix.** `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` now also calls `clearRecentUsers()`. Scoped to this function only, not `resetDatabase()` — that one deliberately keeps the current account's `users`/`stores` rows (it only clears transactional data), so its `recentUsers` entry is still valid and shouldn't be purged.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/clear-database-for-new-store-clears-recent-users.test.ts` — seeds a `users` row and a matching `recentUsers` cache entry, runs the real `clearDatabaseForNewStore()` against a real sql.js database, and asserts both are gone afterward. Confirmed RED by reverting the fix and re-running.

### A-169 — crash `content` had no length cap at all, so the crashes that most needed reporting were the ones that could never be delivered
- **Found:** live, in Sentry — several `Sync item stuck after 5 attempts on feedback/<id>: SQLSTATE[22001]: String data, right truncated: 1406 Data too long for column 'content'` issues against production, most recently 2026-10-06.
- **Root cause.** A-149 capped `fingerprint` and `MAX_CRASH_MESSAGE_LENGTH` capped the message, but `logCrash()` built `content` by interpolating the **raw** `stack` and a **raw** `JSON.stringify(context)`. The server's `feedback.content` is a MySQL `TEXT` column: 65,535 **bytes**. A stack-overflow crash (`RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`) carries a stack far past that, as does any context holding a payload. The push failed, the item retried to its 5-attempt ceiling, and the report was never delivered — so the most severe class of crash was precisely the class that could not be reported. The one metric that would have shown it (`feedback` push failures) was itself the thing failing.
- **Why the fingerprint fix did not cover it.** A-149 reasoned about one column and widened it server-side. `content` is a different column with a different limit, and a byte limit rather than a character one, so nothing about that fix generalised.
- **Fix.** `MAX_CRASH_CONTENT_LENGTH` (16,000), `MAX_CRASH_STACK_LENGTH` (8,000) and `MAX_CRASH_CONTEXT_LENGTH` (2,000) in `client/lib/utils/error-truncation.ts`, applied by a new `buildCrashContent()` in `error-logger.ts`. The character cap is a **quarter** of the byte limit, because a character costs up to 4 bytes in `utf8mb4` and a JavaScript string's `length` says nothing about its encoded size — any cap above 16,383 fails for some input. Per-part caps exist so one oversized part cannot crowd the rest out of the report. Truncating client-side was chosen over widening the column to `MEDIUMTEXT`: a 64KB stack is not worth storing or syncing, and widening would still have left a limit to overflow.
- **A second defect in the first draft of this fix.** Capping the built content then appending the coalescing branch's `(Repeated N times, most recently …)` line put the row back over the cap, and truncating afterwards silently ate the occurrence line — SF-CRASH-1's growth shape in miniature. `withOccurrenceLine()` now reserves the line's budget before truncating, so the count survives on an arbitrarily deep stack.
- **New helper, deliberately distinct from `truncateForLog()`.** That function appends the marker *past* `maxLength`, so its result is `maxLength + 12` — fine for an embedded excerpt, wrong for a fixed-width column. `truncateToLimit()` accounts for the marker inside the budget, so its output can go straight into one.
- **Regression coverage:** `client/__tests__/crash-report-dedup.test.ts` — a 5,000-frame stack plus a 40,000-character context stays under the cap and under 65,535 encoded bytes; the message and the top stack frame survive truncation (a truncated report must still be diagnosable, not a stub); and three repeats of a deep crash stay capped *and* keep their `(Repeated 3 times` line. Confirmed RED before the fix.

### A-174 — the `migrate-db` endpoint reseeded production on every call and reported its own failures as HTTP 200
- **Found:** 2026-10-07, while deciding whether CI could call this endpoint to close A-170.
- **Root cause.** `routes/web.php`'s `GET /migrate-db` ran `migrate --seed --force`, so `DatabaseSeeder` — which *creates a super-admin account* — fired on every production migration; its `catch` returned the string `'Error: …'` with a **200** status, so any caller checking the status code was told a failed migration had succeeded; and the shared secret rode in a query string, landing in the host's access logs and browser history.
- **Fix.** Deleted outright and replaced by the Phase 5 Maintenance page: `POST /api/v1/admin/maintenance/migrations/run` (`role:super_admin`) → `AdminMaintenanceService::runPendingMigrations()`, which runs `migrate --force` and nothing else, returns a non-2xx on failure, caps the migrator output, and writes a `MIGRATIONS_RUN` activity-log entry on **both** success and failure. `MIGRATE_DB_KEY` is gone from `config/app.php` and `.env.example`.
- **The regression this fix nearly introduced.** Dropping `--seed` would have silently ended the only path by which a newly declared permission reached production, since `RolesAndPermissionsSeeder` ran on every old `/migrate-db` hit — `DatabaseSeeder`'s own comments depend on that. Rather than restore the bundling, roles/permissions got their own separately-confirmed action (`POST /maintenance/roles/sync` → `db:seed --class=RolesAndPermissionsSeeder --force`, audited `ROLES_PERMISSIONS_SYNCED`) that invokes that seeder alone and never `DatabaseSeeder`. `SeederIdempotencyTest`'s and `DatabaseSeederTest`'s header comments, which documented the old path as production's, were rewritten in the same change.
- **A second bug this fix shipped green, caught only by a browser smoke test.** `AdminMaintenanceService` originally constructor-injected `Illuminate\Database\Migrations\Migrator`. Every one of the 16 backend tests passed, and the page 500'd on the first real page load: `MigrationServiceProvider` is **deferred**, so auto-wiring the concrete class fails on the unbound `MigrationRepositoryInterface` — but PHPUnit boots Artisan, which registers that provider, so the harness could not reproduce production. Fixed by resolving through `app('migrator')`, guarded by a source-level assertion (`AdminMaintenanceStatusTest::test_the_migrator_is_resolved_by_alias_not_constructor_injected`) for the same reason `PaymentProviderTimeoutTest` is written that way. This is `.agents/AGENTS.md` §9's "backend verification isn't UI verification" exactly as described.
- **Regression coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/Admin/AdminMaintenanceEndpointGateTest.php` (super_admin-only on all three endpoints for `platform_admin`/`agent`/a custom `manage_platform` role, with the custom-role test asserting it clears the outer group gate first; `/migrate-db` now 404s), `AdminMaintenanceRunTest.php` (the invocation asserted to be `migrate --force` with no `--seed`; the roles sync asserted to invoke `RolesAndPermissionsSeeder` and never `DatabaseSeeder`; audit entries on both success and failure — the failure case confirmed RED by returning early from the `catch`), `AdminMaintenanceStatusTest.php` (unknown ≠ zero pending, destructive detection, the alias guard — three confirmed RED by reverting the implementation). Verified end to end in a browser against a local API: 3 pending migrations listed, applied, output and applied-list rendered, pending list re-read to empty, `MIGRATIONS_RUN` logged with the actor; and a `platform_admin` deep-linking to `/admin/maintenance` got the refusal with **zero** requests to the endpoint.

### A-170 — production's schema sat behind `main` for weeks because nothing in the pipeline ran migrations
- **Found:** 2026-10-07, triaging Sentry: `Unknown column 'occurrence_count'` (567 events, 4 users) plus the A-149 `fingerprint` 1406 family, all still firing although their migrations were on `origin/main`.
- **Root cause.** The backend deploys by FTP, so the workflow uploads files and stops; production's schema only advanced when somebody manually hit `GET /migrate-db?key=…`. Nobody did. `laravel-server/AGENTS.md` had documented this exact mechanism, and warned "don't assume 'I wrote the migration' means 'it's live'", the whole time — the gap survived being correctly documented, which is why the fix had to be a mechanism rather than a reminder.
- **Fix.** Phase 5's Maintenance page (`/admin/maintenance`) lists pending migrations, flags the ones that alter existing data, and applies them with an audit trail, plus a "migrations pending" card on `/admin/operations` so drift is visible without being asked for. **Production was then migrated by the owner and reports zero pending (confirmed 2026-10-07).** The `GET /migrate-db` route is gone (A-174).
- **Still true, and worth carrying forward:** the schema being fixed does **not** un-stick rows already parked. A sync item that hit the client's 5-attempt ceiling stops retrying, so reports rejected while the column was narrow stay undelivered even now that the server would accept them.

### PG-18 — a store that synced nothing contributed nothing, so the platform success rate read 100%
- **Found:** code review of admin Phase 2; fixed 2026-10-07 as Phase 2 of the stuck-data work.
- **Root cause.** `SyncController::push()` has two exits that return before the recording hook, which sits after the outer commit: `validateSync()` refusing outright (plan lost `cloud_sync`, interval throttle, store limit), and the outer `catch` returning 500. Neither tallied anything. A store whose every push was refused therefore produced **zero** rows in `sync_health_daily` — and a success rate computed over "pushes we heard about" read 100% while that store synced nothing at all. These are failures that reached the server and were lost there, which is exactly what an operator checking a sync metric is looking for.
- **Fix.** `SyncFailureRecorder::recordRejectedPush()` records the whole attempt as one refusal — a `sync_failures` row carrying *why*, plus a tally with `pushes = 1` and the change count as refused. Called from both exits. Four whole-request reasons joined the canonical list (`sync_disabled`, `sync_throttled`, `store_limit_exceeded`, `server_error`) so these stay out of the unmapped-reason fallback.
- **The outer catch was also widened from `\Exception` to `\Throwable`.** An `\Error` — a TypeError in a payload handler, say — was not caught at all, so the request died with neither a rollback nor any record. That is the exact shape PG-18 described as "every push dies on an `\Error`".
- **Regression coverage:** `laravel-server/tests/Feature/SyncPushRefusedAttemptIsRecordedTest.php` — a plan refusal is tallied as an attempt rather than vanishing, its reason is recorded against the store, and a push that does reach the hook is still counted exactly once (not double-counted by the new path). Confirmed RED on the first two.

### A-176a — a soft-deleted product's movements were pulled without its batches, stranding their deltas forever
- **Found:** 2026-10-07, in review of the A-173 work; one of the mechanisms behind a single store reporting different stock on different devices.
